         - cell [ref=e539]:
          - list [ref=e541]:
           - listitem [ref=e542]:
            - link "v" [ref=e543]:
            - text: ·

            - link "t" [ref=e545]:

            - link "e" [ref=e547]:
      - paragraph [ref=e548]:
       - text: Python is a
       - link "high-level" [ref=e549]:
       - text: ","
       - link "general-purpose programming language" [ref=e550]:
       - text: that emphasizes
       - link "code readability" [ref=e551]:
       - text: ", simplicity, and ease-of-writing with the use of"
       - link "significant indentation" [ref=e552]:
       - text: ","
       - superscript [ref=e553]:
        - link "[38]" [ref=e554]:
       - text: an extensive ("batteries-included")
       - link "standard library" [ref=e556]:
       - text: ", and"
       - link "garbage collection" [ref=e557]:
       - text: . Python supports multiple
       - link "programming paradigms" [ref=e558]:
       - text: but with an emphasis on
       - link "object-oriented programming" [ref=e559]:
       - text: and
       - link "dynamic typing" [ref=e560]:
       - text: .
      - paragraph [ref=e561]:
       - link "Guido van Rossum" [ref=e562]:
       - text: began working on Python in the late 1980s as a successor to the
       - link "ABC" [ref=e563]:
       - text: programming language. Python 3.0, released in 2008, was a major revision and not completely
       - link "backward-compatible" [ref=e564]:
       - text: with earlier versions. Beginning with Python 3.5,
       - superscript [ref=e565]:
        - link "[39]" [ref=e566]:
       - text: capabilities and keywords for typing were added to the language, allowing optional
       - link "static typing" [ref=e568]:
       - text: .
       - superscript [ref=e569]:
        - link "[40]" [ref=e570]:
       - text: As of 2026 , the
       - link "Python Software Foundation" [ref=e572]:
       - text: supports Python 3.11, 3.12, 3.13, 3.14, and 3.15, following the project's annual release cycle and five-year support policy. Python 3.15.0 the latest stable version defaults to
       - link "UTF-8" [ref=e573]:
       - text: ", unlike previous versions. Earlier versions in the 3.x series have reached end-of-life and no longer receive security updates."
      - paragraph [ref=e574]:
       - text: Python is widely taught as an introductory programming language.
       - superscript [ref=e575]:
        - link "[41]" [ref=e576]:
     - region [ref=e578]:
       - heading "History" [level=2] [ref=e580]
       - link "edit" [ref=e582]:
      - note [ref=e583]:
       - text: "Main article:"
       - link "History of Python" [ref=e584]:
      - figure [ref=e585]:
       - link [ref=e586]:
        - text: The designer of Python,
        - link "Guido van Rossum" [ref=e589]:
        - text: ", at"
        - link "PyCon" [ref=e590]:
        - text: US 2024
      - paragraph [ref=e591]:
       - text: Python was conceived in the late 1980s
       - superscript [ref=e592]:
        - link "[11]" [ref=e593]:
       - text: by
       - link "Guido van Rossum" [ref=e595]:
       - text: at
       - link "Centrum Wiskunde & Informatica" [ref=e596]:
       - text: (CWI) in the
       - link "Netherlands" [ref=e597]:
       - text: .
       - superscript [ref=e598]:
        - link "[42]" [ref=e599]:
       - text: It was designed as a successor to the
       - link "ABC" [ref=e601]:
       - text: programming language, which was inspired by
       - link "SETL" [ref=e602]:
       - text: ","
       - superscript [ref=e603]:
        - link "[43]" [ref=e604]:
       - text: capable of
       - link "exception handling" [ref=e606]:
       - text: and interfacing with the
       - link "Amoeba" [ref=e607]:
       - text: operating system.
       - superscript [ref=e608]:
        - link "[18]" [ref=e609]:
       - text: Python implementation began in December 1989.
       - superscript [ref=e611]:
        - link "[42]" [ref=e612]:
       - text: Van Rossum first released it in 1991 as Python 0.9.0.
       - superscript [ref=e614]:
        - link "[42]" [ref=e615]:
       - text: Van Rossum assumed sole responsibility for the project, as the lead developer, until 12 July 2018, when he announced his "permanent vacation" from responsibilities as Python's "
       - link "benevolent dictator for life" [ref=e617]:
       - text: "\" (BDFL); this title was bestowed on him by the Python community to reflect his long-term commitment as the project's chief decision-maker."
       - superscript [ref=e618]:
        - link "[44]" [ref=e619]:
       - superscript [ref=e621]:
        - link "[c]" [ref=e622]:
       - text: In January 2019, active Python core developers elected a five-member Steering Council to lead the project.
       - superscript [ref=e624]:
        - link "[45]" [ref=e625]:
       - superscript [ref=e627]:
        - link "[46]" [ref=e628]:
      - paragraph [ref=e630]:
       - text: The name Python derives from the British comedy series
       - link "Monty Python's Flying Circus" [ref=e632]:
       - text: .
       - superscript [ref=e633]:
        - link "[47]" [ref=e634]:
       - text: (See
       - link "§ Naming" [ref=e636]:
       - text: .)
      - paragraph [ref=e637]:
       - text: Python 2.0 was released on 16 October 2000, featuring many new features such as
       - link "list comprehensions" [ref=e638]:
       - text: ","
       - link "cycle-detecting" [ref=e639]:
       - text: garbage collection,
       - link "reference counting" [ref=e640]:
       - text: ", and"
       - link "Unicode" [ref=e641]:
       - text: support.
       - superscript [ref=e642]:
        - link "[48]" [ref=e643]:
       - text: Python 2.7's
       - link "end-of-life" [ref=e645]:
       - text: was initially set for 2015, and then postponed to 2020 out of concern that a large body of existing code could not easily be forward-ported to Python 3.
       - superscript [ref=e646]:
        - link "[49]" [ref=e647]:
       - superscript [ref=e649]:
        - link "[50]" [ref=e650]:
       - text: It no longer receives security patches or updates.
       - superscript [ref=e652]:
        - link "[51]" [ref=e653]:
       - superscript [ref=e655]:
        - link "[52]" [ref=e656]:
       - text: While Python 2.7 and older versions are officially unsupported, a different unofficial Python implementation,
       - link "PyPy" [ref=e658]:
       - text: ", continues to support Python 2, i.e., \"2.7.18+\" (plus 3.11 and 3.12), with the plus signifying (at least some) \""
       - link "backported" [ref=e659]:
       - text: security updates".
       - superscript [ref=e660]:
        - link "[53]" [ref=e661]:
      - paragraph [ref=e663]:
       - text: Python 3.0 was released on 3 December 2008, and was a major revision and not completely
       - link "backward-compatible" [ref=e664]:
       - text: with earlier versions, with some new semantics and changed syntax. Python 2.7.18, released in 2020, was the last release of Python 2.
       - superscript [ref=e665]:
        - link "[54]" [ref=e666]:
       - text: Several releases in the Python 3.x series have added new syntax to the language, and made a few (considered very minor) backward-incompatible changes.
      - paragraph [ref=e668]:
       - text: As of October 2026 , Python 3.15.0 is the latest stable release, and before in same month 3.14.8 an "expedited security release"
       - superscript [ref=e669]:
        - link "[55]" [ref=e670]:
       - text: is the latest stable release, and since 3.14 official Android binary releases are available. All older 3.x versions had a security update down to Python 3.10.22. Python 3.11 is, since October 2026, the oldest supported branch.
       - superscript [ref=e672]:
        - link "[56]" [ref=e673]:
       - text: Python 3.15 adds e.g.
       - code [ref=e675]: frozendict
       - text: and
       - code [ref=e676]: sentinel
       - text: built-in types, and a new soft keyword,
       - code [ref=e677]: lazy
       - text: ", for lazy imports."
       - superscript [ref=e678]:
        - link "[57]" [ref=e679]:
       - text: Releases receive two years of full support followed by three years of security support.
     - region [ref=e681]:
       - heading "Design philosophy and features" [level=2] [ref=e683]
       - link "edit" [ref=e685]:
      - paragraph [ref=e686]:
       - text: Python is a
       - link "multi-paradigm programming language" [ref=e687]:
       - text: . Object-oriented programming and structured programming are fully supported, and many of their features support functional programming and
       - link "aspect-oriented programming" [ref=e688]:
       - text: – including
       - link "metaprogramming" [ref=e689]:
       - superscript [ref=e690]:
        - link "[58]" [ref=e691]:
       - text: and
       - link "metaobjects" [ref=e693]:
       - text: .
       - superscript [ref=e694]:
        - link "[59]" [ref=e695]:
       - text: Many other paradigms are supported via extensions, including
       - link "design by contract" [ref=e697]:
       - superscript [ref=e698]:
        - link "[60]" [ref=e699]:
       - superscript [ref=e701]:
        - link "[61]" [ref=e702]:
       - text: and
       - link "logic programming" [ref=e704]:
       - text: .
       - superscript [ref=e705]:
        - link "[62]" [ref=e706]:
       - text: Python is often referred to as a
        - text: ‘
        - link "glue language" [ref=e710]:
        - text: ’
       - superscript [ref=e711]:
        - link "[63]" [ref=e712]:
       - text: because it is purposely designed to be able to integrate components written in other languages.
      - paragraph [ref=e714]:
       - text: Python uses dynamic typing and a combination of
       - link "reference counting" [ref=e715]:
       - text: and a cycle-detecting garbage collector for
       - link "memory management" [ref=e716]:
       - text: .
       - superscript [ref=e717]:
        - link "[64]" [ref=e718]:
       - text: It uses dynamic
       - link "name resolution" [ref=e720]:
       - text: (
       - link "late binding" [ref=e721]:
       - text: ), which binds method and variable names during program execution.
      - paragraph [ref=e722]:
       - text: Python's design offers some support for functional programming in the "
       - link "Lisp" [ref=e723]:
       - text: tradition". It has
       - code [ref=e724]: filter
       - text: ","
       - code [ref=e725]: map
       - text: ", and"
       - code [ref=e726]: reduce
       - text: functions;
       - link "list comprehensions" [ref=e727]:
       - text: ","
       - link "dictionaries" [ref=e728]:
       - text: ","
       - link "sets" [ref=e729]:
       - text: ", and"
       - link "generator" [ref=e730]:
       - text: expressions.
       - superscript [ref=e731]:
        - link "[65]" [ref=e732]:
       - text: The standard library has two modules (
       - code [ref=e734]: itertools
       - text: and
       - code [ref=e735]: functools
       - text: ) that implement functional tools borrowed from
       - link "Haskell" [ref=e736]:
       - text: and
       - link "Standard ML" [ref=e737]:
       - text: .
       - superscript [ref=e738]:
        - link "[66]" [ref=e739]:
      - paragraph [ref=e741]:
       - text: Python's core philosophy is summarized in the
       - link "Zen of Python" [ref=e742]:
       - text: (PEP 20) written by
       - link "Tim Peters" [ref=e743]:
       - text: ", which includes aphorisms such as these:"
       - superscript [ref=e744]:
        - link "[67]" [ref=e745]:
      - list [ref=e747]:
       - listitem [ref=e748]: Explicit is better than implicit.
       - listitem [ref=e749]: Simple is better than complex.
       - listitem [ref=e750]: Readability counts.
       - listitem [ref=e751]: Special cases aren't special enough to break the rules.
       - listitem [ref=e752]: Although practicality beats purity, errors should never pass silently, unless explicitly silenced.
       - listitem [ref=e753]: There should be one—and preferably only one—obvious way to do it.
      - paragraph [ref=e754]:
       - text: However, Python has received criticism for violating these principles and adding unnecessary language bloat.
       - superscript [ref=e755]:
        - link "[68]" [ref=e756]:
       - text: Responses to these criticisms note that the Zen of Python is a guideline rather than a rule.
       - superscript [ref=e758]:
        - link "[69]" [ref=e759]:
       - text: "The addition of some new features had been controversial: Guido van Rossum resigned as Benevolent Dictator for Life after conflict about adding the assignment expression operator in Python 3.8."
       - superscript [ref=e761]:
        - link "[70]" [ref=e762]:
       - superscript [ref=e764]:
        - link "[71]" [ref=e765]:
      - paragraph [ref=e767]:
       - text: Nevertheless, rather than building all functionality into its core, Python was designed to be highly
       - link "extensible" [ref=e768]:
       - text: through modules. This compact modularity has made it particularly popular as a means of adding programmable interfaces to existing applications. Van Rossum's vision of a small core language with a large standard library and an easily extensible interpreter stemmed from his frustrations with ABC, which represented the opposite approach.
       - superscript [ref=e769]:
        - link "[11]" [ref=e770]:
      - paragraph [ref=e772]:
       - text: Python claims to strive for a simpler, less-cluttered
       - link "syntax" [ref=e773]:
       - text: and grammar, while giving developers a choice in their coding methodology. Python lacks
       - link [ref=e774]:
        - code [ref=e775]: do .. while
        - text: loops
       - text: ", which"
       - link "Rossum" [ref=e776]:
       - text: considered harmful.
       - superscript [ref=e777]:
        - link "[72]" [ref=e778]:
       - text: In contrast to
       - link "Perl" [ref=e780]:
       - text: "'s motto \""
       - link "there is more than one way to do it" [ref=e781]:
       - text: "\", Python advocates an approach where \"there should be one – and preferably only one – obvious way to do it\"."
       - superscript [ref=e782]:
        - link "[67]" [ref=e783]:
       - text: In practice, however, Python provides many ways to achieve a given goal. There are at least three ways to format a string literal, with no certainty as to which one a programmer should use.
       - superscript [ref=e785]:
        - link "[73]" [ref=e786]:
       - link "Alex Martelli" [ref=e788]:
       - text: is a
       - link "Fellow" [ref=e789]:
       - text: at the
       - link "Python Software Foundation" [ref=e790]:
       - text: and Python book author; he wrote that "To describe something as 'clever' is not considered a compliment in the Python culture."
       - superscript [ref=e791]:
        - link "[74]" [ref=e792]:
      - paragraph [ref=e794]:
       - text: Python's developers typically prioritize readability over performance. For example, they reject patches to non-critical parts of the
       - link "CPython" [ref=e795]:
       - text: reference implementation that would offer increases in speed that do not justify the cost of clarity and readability.
       - superscript [ref=e796]:
        - link "[75]" [ref=e797]:
       - superscript [ref=e799]:
        - text: "["
        - link "failed verification" [ref=e801]:
        - text: "]"
       - text: Execution speed can be improved by moving speed-critical functions to extension modules written in languages such as
       - link "C" [ref=e802]:
       - text: ", or by using a"
       - link "just-in-time compiler" [ref=e803]:
       - text: like
       - link "PyPy" [ref=e804]:
       - text: . Also, it is possible to transpile to other languages. However, this approach either fails to achieve the expected speed-up, since Python is a very
       - link "dynamic language" [ref=e805]:
       - text: ", or only a restricted subset of Python is compiled (with potential minor semantic changes)."
       - superscript [ref=e806]:
        - link "[76]" [ref=e807]:
      - paragraph [ref=e809]:
       - text: Python is meant to be a fun language to use.
       - superscript [ref=e810]:
        - link "[77]" [ref=e811]:
       - superscript [ref=e813]:
        - 'generic "Page / location: 3" [ref=e814]': ": 3"
       - text: This goal is reflected in the name – a tribute to the British comedy group
       - link "Monty Python" [ref=e815]:
       - superscript [ref=e816]:
        - link "[78]" [ref=e817]:
       - text: – and in playful approaches to some tutorials and reference materials. For instance, some code examples use the terms "spam" and "eggs" (in reference to
       - link "a Monty Python sketch" [ref=e819]:
       - text: ), rather than the typical terms
       - link "\"foo\" and \"bar\"" [ref=e820]:
       - text: .
       - superscript [ref=e821]:
        - link "[77]" [ref=e822]:
       - superscript [ref=e824]:
        - link "[79]" [ref=e825]:
      - paragraph [ref=e827]:
       - text: A common
       - link "neologism" [ref=e828]:
       - text: "in the Python community is pythonic, which has a broad range of meanings related to program style: Pythonic code may use Python"
       - link "idioms" [ref=e829]:
       - text: well; be natural or show fluency in the language; or conform with Python's minimalist philosophy and emphasis on readability.
       - superscript [ref=e830]:
        - link "[80]" [ref=e831]:
      - region [ref=e833]:
        - heading "Enhancement Proposals" [level=3] [ref=e835]
        - link "edit" [ref=e837]:
       - paragraph [ref=e838]:
        - text: Python Enhancement Proposals
        - superscript [ref=e839]:
         - link "[note 1]" [ref=e840]:
        - text: are a design document for either providing information to the
        - link "Python" [ref=e842]:
        - text: community, or proposal for new feature in Python.
        - superscript [ref=e843]:
         - link "[81]" [ref=e844]:
        - text: PEPs are intended to explain new processes in Python, provide
        - link "naming conventions" [ref=e846]:
        - text: or document the processes in the language.
        - superscript [ref=e847]:
         - link "[82]" [ref=e848]:
        - text: PEPs are overseen by Python Steering Council.
        - superscript [ref=e850]:
         - link "[82]" [ref=e851]:
       - paragraph [ref=e853]:
        - text: There are 3 kinds of PEPs, with those are being standards track PEP
        - superscript [ref=e854]:
         - link "[note 2]" [ref=e855]:
        - text: ", Informational PEP"
        - superscript [ref=e857]:
         - link "[note 3]" [ref=e858]:
        - text: and Process PEPs
        - superscript [ref=e860]:
         - link "[note 4]" [ref=e861]:
        - text: which has their own unique meanings.
        - superscript [ref=e863]:
         - link "[81]" [ref=e864]:
        - superscript [ref=e866]:
         - link "[83]" [ref=e867]:
        - text: They were firstly introduced in 2000, inspired by other RfCs (requests for comments) and Design Enhancement Proposals.
        - superscript [ref=e869]:
         - link "[83]" [ref=e870]:
        - text: Most known PEPs are PEP – 1, PEP – 8,
        - link "PEP – 20" [ref=e872]:
        - text: ", PEP – 257 and others."
        - superscript [ref=e873]:
         - link "[83]" [ref=e874]:
     - region [ref=e876]:
       - heading "Syntax and semantics" [level=2] [ref=e878]
       - link "edit" [ref=e880]:
      - note [ref=e881]:
       - text: "Main article:"
       - link "Python syntax and semantics" [ref=e882]:
      - paragraph [ref=e883]:
       - text: Python is meant to be an easily readable language. Its formatting is visually uncluttered and often uses English keywords where other languages use punctuation. Unlike many other languages, it does not use
       - link "curly brackets" [ref=e884]:
       - text: to delimit blocks, and semicolons after statements are allowed but rarely used. It has fewer syntactic exceptions and special cases than
       - link "C" [ref=e885]:
       - text: or
       - link "Pascal" [ref=e886]:
       - text: .
       - superscript [ref=e887]:
        - link "[84]" [ref=e888]:
      - region [ref=e890]:
        - heading "Indentation" [level=3] [ref=e892]
        - link "edit" [ref=e894]:
       - note [ref=e895]:
        - text: "Further information:"
        - link "Python syntax and semantics § Indentation" [ref=e896]:
       - paragraph [ref=e897]:
        - text: Python uses
        - link "whitespace" [ref=e898]:
        - text: indentation, rather than curly brackets or keywords, to delimit
        - link "blocks" [ref=e899]:
        - text: . An increase in indentation comes after certain statements; a decrease in indentation signifies the end of the current block.
        - superscript [ref=e900]:
         - link "[85]" [ref=e901]:
        - text: Thus, the program's visual structure accurately represents its semantic structure.
        - superscript [ref=e903]:
         - link "[86]" [ref=e904]:
        - text: This feature is sometimes termed the
        - link "off-side rule" [ref=e906]:
        - text: . Some other languages use indentation this way; but in most, indentation has no semantic meaning. The recommended indent size is four spaces.
        - superscript [ref=e907]:
         - link "[87]" [ref=e908]:
      - region [ref=e910]:
        - heading "Statements and control flow" [level=3] [ref=e912]
        - link "edit" [ref=e914]:
       - paragraph [ref=e915]:
        - text: Python's
        - link "statements" [ref=e916]:
        - text: "include the following:"
       - list [ref=e917]:
        - listitem [ref=e918]:
         - text: The
         - link "assignment" [ref=e919]:
         - text: statement, using a single equals sign
         - code [ref=e920]: =
        - listitem [ref=e921]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e922]:
          - link "if" [ref=e923]:
         - text: statement, which conditionally executes a block of code, along with
         - code [ref=e924]:
          - link "else" [ref=e925]:
         - text: and
         - code [ref=e926]: elif
         - text: (a contraction of
         - code [ref=e927]:
          - link "else if" [ref=e928]:
         - text: )
        - listitem [ref=e929]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e930]:
          - link "for" [ref=e931]:
         - text: statement, which iterates over an iterable object, capturing each element to a variable for use by the attached block; the variable is not deleted when the loop finishes
        - listitem [ref=e932]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e933]:
          - link "while" [ref=e934]:
         - text: statement, which executes a block of code as long as boolean condition is true
        - listitem [ref=e935]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e936]:
          - link "try" [ref=e937]:
         - text: statement, which allows exceptions raised in its attached code block to be caught and handled by
         - code [ref=e938]: except
         - text: clauses (or new syntax
         - code [ref=e939]: except*
         - text: in Python 3.11 for exception groups);
         - superscript [ref=e940]:
          - link "[88]" [ref=e941]:
         - text: the
         - code [ref=e943]: try
         - text: statement also ensures that clean-up code in a
         - code [ref=e944]: finally
         - text: block is always run regardless of how the block exits
        - listitem [ref=e945]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e946]: raise
         - text: statement, used to raise a specified exception or re-raise a caught exception
        - listitem [ref=e947]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e948]: class
         - text: statement, which executes a block of code and attaches its local namespace to a
         - link "class" [ref=e949]:
         - text: ", for use in object-oriented programming"
        - listitem [ref=e950]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e951]: def
         - text: statement, which defines a
         - link "function" [ref=e952]:
         - text: or
         - link "method" [ref=e953]:
        - listitem [ref=e954]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e955]:
          - link "with" [ref=e956]:
         - text: statement, which encloses a code block within a context manager, allowing
         - link "resource-acquisition-is-initialization" [ref=e957]:
         - text: (RAII)-like behavior and replacing a common try/finally idiom
         - superscript [ref=e958]:
          - link "[89]" [ref=e959]:
         - text: Examples of a context include acquiring a
         - link "lock" [ref=e961]:
         - text: before some code is run, and then releasing the lock; or opening and then closing a
         - link "file" [ref=e962]:
        - listitem [ref=e963]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e964]:
          - link "break" [ref=e965]:
         - text: statement, which exits a loop
        - listitem [ref=e966]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e967]: continue
         - text: statement, which skips the rest of the current iteration and continues with the next
        - listitem [ref=e968]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e969]: del
         - text: statement, which removes a variable—deleting the reference from the name to the value, and producing an error if the variable is referred to before it is redefined
         - superscript [ref=e970]:
          - link "[d]" [ref=e971]:
        - listitem [ref=e973]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e974]: pass
         - text: statement, serving as a
         - link "NOP" [ref=e975]:
         - text: (i.e., no operation), which is syntactically needed to create an empty code block
        - listitem [ref=e976]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e977]:
          - link "assert" [ref=e978]:
         - text: statement, used in debugging to check for conditions that should apply
        - listitem [ref=e979]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e980]: yield
         - text: statement, which returns a value from a
         - link "generator" [ref=e981]:
         - text: function (and also an operator); used to implement
         - link "coroutines" [ref=e982]:
        - listitem [ref=e983]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e984]: return
         - text: statement, used to return a value from a function
        - listitem [ref=e985]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e986]:
          - link "import" [ref=e987]:
         - text: and
         - code [ref=e988]: from
         - text: "statements, used to import modules whose functions or variables can be used in the current program. Python 3.15 adds a new functionality to lazily import with a new keyword: \"The"
         - code [ref=e989]: lazy
         - text: keyword works with both
         - code [ref=e990]: import
         - text: and
         - code [ref=e991]: from ... import
         - text: statements."
         - superscript [ref=e992]:
          - link "[90]" [ref=e993]:
        - listitem [ref=e995]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e996]: match
         - text: and
         - code [ref=e997]: case
         - text: statements, analogous to a
         - link "switch statement" [ref=e998]:
         - text: construct, which compares an expression against one or more cases as a control-flow measure
       - paragraph [ref=e999]:
        - text: The assignment statement (
        - code [ref=e1000]: =
        - text: ) binds a name as a
        - link "reference" [ref=e1001]:
        - text: to a separate, dynamically allocated
        - link "object" [ref=e1002]:
        - text: . Variables may subsequently be rebound at any time to any object. In Python, a variable name is a generic reference holder without a fixed
        - link "data type" [ref=e1003]:
        - text: ; however, it always refers to some object with a type. This is called
        - link "dynamic typing" [ref=e1004]:
        - text: —in contrast to
        - link "statically-typed" [ref=e1005]:
        - text: languages, where each variable may contain only a value of a certain type.
       - paragraph [ref=e1006]:
        - text: Python does not support
        - link "tail call" [ref=e1007]:
        - text: optimization or
        - link "first-class continuations" [ref=e1008]:
        - text: ; according to Van Rossum, the language never will.
        - superscript [ref=e1009]:
         - link "[91]" [ref=e1010]:
        - superscript [ref=e1012]:
         - link "[92]" [ref=e1013]:
        - text: However, better support for
        - link "coroutine" [ref=e1015]:
        - text: "-like functionality is provided by extending Python's generators."
        - superscript [ref=e1016]:
         - link "[93]" [ref=e1017]:
        - text: Before 2.5, generators were
        - link "lazy" [ref=e1019]:
        - link "iterators" [ref=e1020]:
        - text: ; data was passed unidirectionally out of the generator. From Python 2.5 on, it is possible to pass data back into a generator function; and from version 3.3, data can be passed through multiple stack levels.
        - superscript [ref=e1021]:
         - link "[94]" [ref=e1022]:
      - region [ref=e1024]:
        - heading "Expressions" [level=3] [ref=e1026]
        - link "edit" [ref=e1028]:
       - paragraph [ref=e1029]:
        - text: Python's
        - link "expressions" [ref=e1030]:
        - text: "include the following:"
       - list [ref=e1031]:
        - listitem [ref=e1032]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e1033]: +
         - text: ","
         - code [ref=e1034]: "-"
         - text: ", and"
         - code [ref=e1035]: "*"
         - text: "operators for mathematical addition, subtraction, and multiplication are similar to other languages, but the behavior of division differs. There are two types of division in Python:"
         - link "floor division" [ref=e1036]:
         - text: (or integer division)
         - code [ref=e1037]: //
         - text: ", and floating-point division"
         - code [ref=e1038]: /
         - text: .
         - superscript [ref=e1039]:
          - link "[95]" [ref=e1040]:
         - text: Python uses the
         - code [ref=e1042]: "**"
         - text: operator for exponentiation.
        - listitem [ref=e1043]:
         - text: Python uses the
         - code [ref=e1044]: +
         - text: operator for string concatenation. The language uses the
         - code [ref=e1045]: "*"
         - text: operator for duplicating a string a specified number of times.
        - listitem [ref=e1046]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e1047]: "@"
         - text: infix operator is intended to be used by libraries such as
         - link "NumPy" [ref=e1048]:
         - text: for
         - link "matrix multiplication" [ref=e1049]:
         - text: .
         - superscript [ref=e1050]:
          - link "[96]" [ref=e1051]:
         - superscript [ref=e1053]:
          - link "[97]" [ref=e1054]:
        - listitem [ref=e1056]:
         - text: The syntax
         - code [ref=e1057]: :=
         - text: ", called the \""
         - generic [ref=e1058]: walrus operator
         - text: "\", was introduced in Python 3.8. This operator assigns values to variables as part of a larger expression."
         - superscript [ref=e1059]:
          - link "[98]" [ref=e1060]:
        - listitem [ref=e1062]:
         - text: In Python,
         - code [ref=e1063]: ==
         - text: compares two objects by value. Python's
         - code [ref=e1064]: is
         - text: operator may be used to compare object identities (i.e., comparison by reference), and comparisons may be chained—for example,
         - code [ref=e1065]: a <= b <= c
         - text: .
        - listitem [ref=e1066]:
         - text: Python uses
         - code [ref=e1067]: and
         - text: ","
         - code [ref=e1068]: or
         - text: ", and"
         - code [ref=e1069]: not
         - text: as Boolean operators.
        - listitem [ref=e1070]:
         - text: Python has a type of expression called a
         - link "list comprehension" [ref=e1072]:
         - text: ", and a more general expression called a generator expression."
         - superscript [ref=e1073]:
          - link "[65]" [ref=e1074]:
        - listitem [ref=e1076]:
         - link "Anonymous functions" [ref=e1077]:
         - text: are implemented using
         - link "lambda expressions" [ref=e1078]:
         - text: ; however, there may be only one expression in each body.
        - listitem [ref=e1079]:
         - text: Conditional expressions are written as
         - code [ref=e1080]: x if c else y
         - text: .
         - superscript [ref=e1081]:
          - link "[99]" [ref=e1082]:
         - text: (This is different in operand order from the
         - code [ref=e1084]:
          - 'link "c ? x : y" [ref=e1085]':
         - text: operator common to many other languages.)
        - listitem [ref=e1086]:
         - text: Python makes a distinction between
         - link "lists" [ref=e1087]:
         - text: and
         - link "tuples" [ref=e1088]:
         - text: . Lists are written as
         - code [ref=e1089]: "[1, 2, 3]"
         - text: ", are mutable, and cannot be used as dictionary keys because they are not hashable. Tuples, written as"
         - code [ref=e1090]: (1, 2, 3)
         - text: ", are immutable and can be used as dictionary keys if all of their elements are hashable."
         - superscript [ref=e1091]:
          - link "[100]" [ref=e1092]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e1094]: +
         - text: operator can be used to concatenate two tuples, which does not directly modify their contents, but produces a new tuple containing the elements of both. For example, given the variable
         - code [ref=e1095]: t
         - text: initially equal to
         - code [ref=e1096]: (1, 2, 3)
         - text: ", executing"
         - code [ref=e1097]: t = t + (4, 5)
         - text: first evaluates
         - code [ref=e1098]: t + (4, 5)
         - text: ", which yields"
         - code [ref=e1099]: (1, 2, 3, 4, 5)
         - text: ; this result is then assigned back to
         - code [ref=e1100]: t
         - text: —thereby effectively "modifying the contents" of
         - code [ref=e1101]: t
         - text: while conforming to the immutable nature of tuple objects. Parentheses are optional for tuples in unambiguous contexts.
         - superscript [ref=e1102]:
          - link "[101]" [ref=e1103]:
        - listitem [ref=e1105]:
         - text: Python features sequence unpacking where multiple expressions, each evaluating to something assignable (e.g., a variable or a writable property) are associated just as in forming tuple literal; as a whole, the results are then put on the left-hand side of the equal sign in an assignment statement. This statement expects an iterable object on the right-hand side of the equal sign to produce the same number of values as the writable expressions on the left-hand side; while iterating, the statement assigns each of the values produced on the right to the corresponding expression on the left.
         - superscript [ref=e1106]:
          - link "[102]" [ref=e1107]:
        - listitem [ref=e1109]:
         - text: Python has a "string format" operator
         - code [ref=e1110]: "%"
         - text: that functions analogously to
         - code [ref=e1111]:
          - link "printf" [ref=e1112]:
         - text: format strings in the C language—e.g.
         - code [ref=e1113]: "\"spam=%s eggs=%d\" % (\"blah\", 2)"
         - text: evaluates to
         - code [ref=e1114]: "\"spam=blah eggs=2\""
         - text: . In Python 2.6+ and 3+, this operator was supplemented by the
         - code [ref=e1115]: format()
         - text: method of the
         - code [ref=e1116]: str
         - text: class, e.g.,
         - code [ref=e1117]: "\"spam={0} eggs={1}\".format(\"blah\", 2)"
         - text: ". Python 3.6 added \"f-strings\":"
         - code [ref=e1118]: "spam = \"blah\"; eggs = 2; f'spam={spam} eggs={eggs}'"
         - text: .
         - superscript [ref=e1119]:
          - link "[103]" [ref=e1120]:
        - listitem [ref=e1122]:
         - text: Strings in Python can be
         - link "concatenated" [ref=e1123]:
         - text: by "adding" them (using the same operator as for adding integers and floats); e.g.,
         - code [ref=e1124]: "\"spam\" + \"eggs\""
         - text: returns
         - code [ref=e1125]: "\"spameggs\""
         - text: . If strings contain numbers, they are concatenated as strings rather than as integers, e.g.
         - code [ref=e1126]: "\"2\" + \"2\""
         - text: returns
         - code [ref=e1127]: "\"22\""
         - text: .
        - listitem [ref=e1128]:
         - text: Python supports
         - link "string literals" [ref=e1129]:
         - text: "in several ways:"
         - list [ref=e1130]:
          - listitem [ref=e1131]:
           - text: Delimited by single or double quotation marks; single and double quotation marks have equivalent functionality (unlike in
           - link "Unix shells" [ref=e1132]:
           - text: ","
           - link "Perl" [ref=e1133]:
           - text: ", and Perl-influenced languages). Both marks use the backslash ("
           - code [ref=e1134]: \
           - text: ) as an
           - link "escape character" [ref=e1135]:
           - text: .
           - link "String interpolation" [ref=e1136]:
           - text: became available in Python 3.6 as "formatted string literals".
           - superscript [ref=e1137]:
            - link "[103]" [ref=e1138]:
          - listitem [ref=e1140]:
           - text: Triple-quoted, i.e., starting and ending with three single or double quotation marks; this may span multiple lines and function like
           - link "here documents" [ref=e1141]:
           - text: in shells, Perl, and
           - link "Ruby" [ref=e1142]:
           - text: .
          - listitem [ref=e1143]:
           - link "Raw string" [ref=e1144]:
           - text: varieties, denoted by prefixing the string literal with
           - code [ref=e1145]: r
           - text: . Escape sequences are not interpreted; hence raw strings are useful where literal backslashes are common, such as in
           - link "regular expressions" [ref=e1146]:
           - text: and
           - link "Windows" [ref=e1147]:
           - text: "-style paths. (Compare \""
           - code [ref=e1148]: "@"
           - text: "-quoting\" in"
           - link "C#" [ref=e1149]:
           - text: .)
        - listitem [ref=e1150]:
         - text: Python has
         - link "array index" [ref=e1151]:
         - text: and
         - link "array slicing" [ref=e1152]:
         - text: expressions in lists, which are written as
         - code [ref=e1153]: a[key]
         - text: ","
         - code [ref=e1154]: a[start:stop]
         - text: or
         - code [ref=e1155]: a[start:stop:step]
         - text: . Indexes are
         - link "zero-based" [ref=e1156]:
         - text: ", and negative indexes are relative to the end. Slices take elements from the start index up to, but not including, the stop index. The (optional) third slice"
         - link "parameter" [ref=e1157]:
         - text: ", called step or stride, allows elements to be skipped or reversed. Slice indexes may be omitted—for example,"
         - code [ref=e1158]: a[:]
         - text: returns a copy of the entire list. Each element of a slice is a
         - link "shallow copy" [ref=e1159]:
         - text: .
       - paragraph [ref=e1160]:
        - text: In Python, a distinction between expressions and statements is rigidly enforced, in contrast to languages such as
        - link "Common Lisp" [ref=e1161]:
        - text: ","
        - link "Scheme" [ref=e1162]:
        - text: ", or"
        - link "Ruby" [ref=e1163]:
        - text: ". This distinction leads to duplicating some functionality, for example:"
       - list [ref=e1164]:
        - listitem [ref=e1165]:
         - link "List comprehensions" [ref=e1166]:
         - text: vs.
         - code [ref=e1167]: for
         - text: "-loops"
        - listitem [ref=e1168]:
         - link "Conditional" [ref=e1169]:
         - text: expressions vs.
         - code [ref=e1170]: if
         - text: blocks
        - listitem [ref=e1171]:
         - text: The
         - code [ref=e1172]: eval()
         - text: vs.
         - code [ref=e1173]: exec()
         - text: built-in functions (in Python 2,
         - code [ref=e1174]: exec
         - text: is a statement); the former function is for expressions, while the latter is for statements
       - paragraph [ref=e1175]:
        - text: A statement cannot be part of an expression; because of this restriction, expressions such as list and
        - code [ref=e1176]: dict
        - text: comprehensions (and lambda expressions) cannot contain statements. As a particular case, an assignment statement such as
        - code [ref=e1177]: a = 1
        - text: cannot be part of the conditional expression of a conditional statement.
      - region [ref=e1178]:
        - heading "Typing" [level=3] [ref=e1180]
        - link "edit" [ref=e1182]:
       - figure "The standard type hierarchy in Python 3" [ref=e1183]:
        - link [ref=e1184]:
       - paragraph [ref=e1187]:
        - text: Python uses
        - link "duck typing" [ref=e1188]:
        - text: ", and it has typed objects but untyped variable names. Type constraints are not checked at definition time; rather, operations on an object may fail at usage time, indicating that the object is not of an appropriate type. Despite being"
        - link "dynamically typed" [ref=e1189]:
        - text: ", Python is"
        - link "strongly typed" [ref=e1190]:
        - text: ", forbidding operations that are poorly defined (e.g., adding a number and a string) rather than quietly attempting to interpret them."
       - paragraph [ref=e1191]:
        - text: Python allows programmers to define their own types using
        - link "classes" [ref=e1192]:
        - text: ", most often for"
        - link "object-oriented programming" [ref=e1193]:
        - text: . New
        - link "instances" [ref=e1194]:
        - text: of classes are constructed by calling the class, for example,
        - code [ref=e1195]: SpamClass()
        - text: or
        - code [ref=e1196]: EggsClass()
        - text: ); the classes are instances of the
        - link "metaclass" [ref=e1197]:
        - code [ref=e1198]: type
        - text: (which is an instance of itself), thereby allowing metaprogramming and
        - link "reflection" [ref=e1199]:
        - text: .
       - paragraph [ref=e1200]:
        - text: "Before version 3.0, Python had two kinds of classes, both using the same syntax: old-style and new-style."
        - superscript [ref=e1201]:
         - link "[104]" [ref=e1202]:
        - text: Current Python versions support the semantics of only the new style.
       - paragraph [ref=e1204]:
        - text: Python supports
        - link "optional type annotations" [ref=e1205]:
        - text: .
        - superscript [ref=e1206]:
         - link "[5]" [ref=e1207]:
        - superscript [ref=e1209]:
         - link "[105]" [ref=e1210]:
        - text: These annotations are not enforced by the language, but may be used by external tools such as mypy to catch errors. Python includes a module
        - code [ref=e1212]: typing
        - text: including several type names for type annotations.
        - superscript [ref=e1213]:
         - link "[106]" [ref=e1214]:
        - superscript [ref=e1216]:
         - link "[107]" [ref=e1217]:
        - text: Also, mypy supports a Python compiler called mypyc, which leverages type annotations for optimization.

      - region [ref=e1414]:
        - heading "Arithmetic operations" [level=3] [ref=e1416]
        - link "edit" [ref=e1418]:
       - paragraph [ref=e1419]:
        - text: Python includes conventional symbols for arithmetic operators (
        - code [ref=e1420]: +
        - text: ","
        - code [ref=e1421]: "-"
        - text: ","
        - code [ref=e1422]: "*"
        - text: ","
        - code [ref=e1423]: /
        - text: ), the floor-division operator
        - code [ref=e1424]: //
        - text: ", and the"
        - link "modulo operator" [ref=e1425]:
        - code [ref=e1426]: "%"
        - text: . (With the modulo operator, a remainder can be negative, e.g.,
        - code [ref=e1427]: 4 % -3 == -2
        - text: .) Python also offers the
        - code [ref=e1428]: "**"
        - text: symbol for
        - link "exponentiation" [ref=e1429]:
        - text: ", e.g."
        - code [ref=e1430]: 5**3 == 125
        - text: and
        - code [ref=e1431]: 9**0.5 == 3.0
        - text: ", as well as the matrix‑multiplication operator"
        - code [ref=e1432]: "@"
        - text: .
        - superscript [ref=e1433]:
         - link "[114]" [ref=e1434]:
        - text: These operators work as in traditional mathematics; with the same
        - link "precedence rules" [ref=e1436]:
        - text: ", the"
        - link "infix" [ref=e1437]:
        - text: operators
        - code [ref=e1438]: +
        - text: and
        - code [ref=e1439]: "-"
        - text: can also be
        - link "unary" [ref=e1440]:
        - text: ", to represent positive and negative numbers respectively."
       - paragraph [ref=e1441]:
        - text: "Division between integers produces floating-point results. The behavior of division has changed significantly over time:"
        - superscript [ref=e1442]:
         - link "[115]" [ref=e1443]:
       - list [ref=e1445]:
        - listitem [ref=e1446]:
         - text: The current version of Python (i.e., since 3.0) changed the
         - code [ref=e1447]: /
         - text: operator to always represent floating-point division, e.g.,
         - code [ref=e1448]: 5/2 == 2.5
         - text: .
        - listitem [ref=e1449]:
         - text: The floor division
         - code [ref=e1450]: //
         - text: operator was introduced, meaning that
         - code [ref=e1451]: 7//3 == 2
         - text: ","
         - code [ref=e1452]: "-7//3 == -3"
         - text: ","
         - code [ref=e1453]: 7.5//3 == 2.0
         - text: ", and"
         - code [ref=e1454]: "-7.5//3 == -3.0"
         - text: . For Python 2.7, adding the
         - code [ref=e1455]: from __future__ import division
         - text: statement allows a module in Python 2.7 to use Python 3.x rules for division (see above).
       - paragraph [ref=e1456]:
        - text: In Python terms, the
        - code [ref=e1457]: /
        - text: operator represents true division (or simply division), while the
        - code [ref=e1458]: //
        - text: operator represents floor division. Before version 3.0, the
        - code [ref=e1459]: /
        - text: operator represents classic division.
        - superscript [ref=e1460]:
         - link "[115]" [ref=e1461]:
       - paragraph [ref=e1463]:
        - link "Rounding" [ref=e1464]:
        - text: towards negative infinity, though a different method than in most languages, adds consistency to Python. For instance, this rounding implies that the equation
        - code [ref=e1465]: (a + b)//b == a//b + 1
        - text: is always true. Also, the rounding implies that the equation
        - code [ref=e1466]: b*(a//b) + a%b == a
        - text: is valid for both positive and negative values of
        - code [ref=e1467]: a
        - text: . As expected, the result of
        - code [ref=e1468]: a%b
        - text: lies in the
        - link "half-open interval" [ref=e1469]:
        - text: "[0, b), where"
        - code [ref=e1470]: b
        - text: is a positive integer; however, maintaining the validity of the equation requires that the result must lie in the interval (b, 0] when
        - code [ref=e1471]: b
        - text: is negative.
        - superscript [ref=e1472]:
         - link "[116]" [ref=e1473]:
       - paragraph [ref=e1475]:
        - text: Python provides a
        - code [ref=e1476]: round
        - text: function for rounding a float to the nearest integer. For
        - link "tie-breaking" [ref=e1477]:
        - text: ", Python 3 uses the round to even method:"
        - code [ref=e1478]: round(1.5)
        - text: and
        - code [ref=e1479]: round(2.5)
        - text: both produce
        - code [ref=e1480]: "2"
        - text: .
        - superscript [ref=e1481]:
         - link "[117]" [ref=e1482]:
        - text: Python versions before 3 used the
        - link "round-away-from-zero" [ref=e1484]:
        - text: "method:"
        - code [ref=e1485]: round(0.5)
        - text: is
        - code [ref=e1486]: "1.0"
        - text: ", and"
        - code [ref=e1487]: round(-0.5)
        - text: is
        - code [ref=e1488]: −1.0
        - text: .
        - superscript [ref=e1489]:
         - link "[118]" [ref=e1490]:
       - paragraph [ref=e1492]:
        - text: Python allows Boolean expressions that contain multiple equality relations to be consistent with general usage in mathematics. For example, the expression
        - code [ref=e1493]: a < b < c
        - text: tests whether
        - code [ref=e1494]: a
        - text: is less than
        - code [ref=e1495]: b
        - text: and
        - code [ref=e1496]: b
        - text: is less than
        - code [ref=e1497]: c
        - text: .
        - superscript [ref=e1498]:
         - link "[119]" [ref=e1499]:
        - text: "C-derived languages interpret this expression differently: in C, the expression would first evaluate"
        - code [ref=e1501]: a < b
        - text: ", resulting in 0 or 1, and that result would then be compared with"
        - code [ref=e1502]: c
        - text: .
        - superscript [ref=e1503]:
         - link "[120]" [ref=e1504]:
       - paragraph [ref=e1506]:
        - text: Python uses
        - link "arbitrary-precision arithmetic" [ref=e1507]:
        - text: for all integer operations. The
        - code [ref=e1508]: Decimal
        - text: type/class in the
        - code [ref=e1509]: decimal
        - text: module provides
        - link "decimal floating-point numbers" [ref=e1510]:
        - text: to a pre-defined arbitrary precision with several rounding modes.
        - superscript [ref=e1511]:
         - link "[121]" [ref=e1512]:
        - text: The
        - code [ref=e1514]: Fraction
        - text: class in the
        - code [ref=e1515]: fractions
        - text: module provides arbitrary precision for
        - link "rational numbers" [ref=e1516]:
        - text: .
        - superscript [ref=e1517]:
         - link "[122]" [ref=e1518]:
       - paragraph [ref=e1520]:
        - text: Due to Python's extensive mathematics library and the third-party library
        - link "NumPy" [ref=e1521]:
        - text: ", the language is frequently used for scientific scripting in tasks such as numerical data processing and manipulation."
        - superscript [ref=e1522]:
         - link "[123]" [ref=e1523]:
        - superscript [ref=e1525]:
         - link "[124]" [ref=e1526]:
      - region [ref=e1528]:
        - heading "Function syntax" [level=3] [ref=e1530]
        - link "edit" [ref=e1532]:
       - paragraph [ref=e1533]:
        - link "Functions" [ref=e1534]:
        - text: are created in Python by using the
        - code [ref=e1535]: def
        - text: "keyword. A function is defined similarly to how it is called, by first providing the function name and then the required parameters. Here is an example of a function that prints its inputs:"
       - generic [ref=e1536]: "def printer(input1, input2 = \"already there\"): print(input1) print(input2) printer(\"hello\") # Example output: # hello # already there"
       - paragraph [ref=e1538]: To assign a default value to a function parameter in case no actual value is provided at run time, variable-definition syntax can be used inside the function header.
     - region [ref=e1539]:
       - heading "Code examples" [level=2] [ref=e1541]
       - link "edit" [ref=e1543]:
      - paragraph [ref=e1544]:
       - link "\"Hello, World!\" program" [ref=e1545]:
       - text: ":"
      - generic [ref=e1546]: print('Hello, World!')
      - paragraph [ref=e1548]:
       - text: Program to calculate the
       - link "factorial" [ref=e1549]:
       - text: "of a non-negative integer:"
       - generic [ref=e1552]: "1"
       - text: "text = input('Type a number, and its factorial will be printed: ')"
       - generic [ref=e1553]: "2"
       - generic [ref=e1554]: "3"
       - text: "if text.isdigit():"
       - generic [ref=e1555]: "4"
       - text: n = int(text)
       - generic [ref=e1556]: "5"
       - text: "else:"
       - generic [ref=e1557]: "6"
       - text: raise TypeError('You must input a number')
       - generic [ref=e1558]: "7"
       - generic [ref=e1559]: "8"
       - text: "if n < 0:"
       - generic [ref=e1560]: "9"
       - text: raise ValueError('You must enter a non-negative integer')
       - generic [ref=e1561]: "10"
       - generic [ref=e1562]: "11"
       - text: factorial = 1
       - generic [ref=e1563]: "12"
       - text: "for i in range(2, n + 1):"
       - generic [ref=e1564]: "13"
       - text: factorial *= i
       - generic [ref=e1565]: "14"
       - generic [ref=e1566]: "15"
       - text: print(factorial)
     - region [ref=e1567]:
       - heading "Libraries" [level=2] [ref=e1569]
       - link "edit" [ref=e1571]:
      - paragraph [ref=e1572]:
       - text: Python's large standard library
       - superscript [ref=e1573]:
        - link "[125]" [ref=e1574]:
       - text: is commonly cited as one of its greatest strengths. For Internet-facing applications, many standard formats and protocols such as
       - link "MIME" [ref=e1576]:
       - text: and
       - link "HTTP" [ref=e1577]:
       - text: are supported. The language includes modules for creating
       - link "graphical user interfaces" [ref=e1578]:
       - text: ", connecting to"
       - link "relational databases" [ref=e1579]:
       - text: ","
       - link "generating pseudorandom numbers" [ref=e1580]:
       - text: ", arithmetic with arbitrary-precision decimals,"
       - superscript [ref=e1581]:
        - link "[121]" [ref=e1582]:
       - text: manipulating
       - link "regular expressions" [ref=e1584]:
       - text: ", and"
       - link "unit testing" [ref=e1585]:
       - text: .
      - paragraph [ref=e1586]:
       - text: Some parts of the standard library are covered by specifications—for example, the
       - link "Web Server Gateway Interface" [ref=e1587]:
       - text: (WSGI) implementation
       - code [ref=e1588]: wsgiref
       - text: follows PEP 333
       - superscript [ref=e1589]:
        - link "[126]" [ref=e1590]:
       - text: —but most parts are specified by their code, internal documentation, and
       - link "test suites" [ref=e1592]:
       - text: . However, because most of the standard library is cross-platform Python code, only a few modules must be altered or rewritten for variant implementations.
      - paragraph [ref=e1593]:
       - text: As of 1 October 2026, the
       - link "Python Package Index" [ref=e1594]:
       - text: (PyPI), the official repository for third-party Python software, contains over 905,649
       - superscript [ref=e1595]:
        - link "[127]" [ref=e1596]:
       - text: projects.
     - region [ref=e1598]:
